# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-20

## Recommendation System
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[AGR: Agentic Generative Retrieval with Tool-Augmented Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26831v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large-scale search and recommendation systems derive much of their retrieval quality from rich, heterogeneous features, yet existing generative retrieval (GR) methods struggle to exploit such feature ecosystems efficiently. Static feature injection either encodes all available signals or places them according to predetermined schemes, increasing serving cost and introducing irrelevant or conflicting evidence. More fundamentally, existing methods cannot adaptively determine when a feature is needed, which feature to select, or where it should influence hierarchical Semantic ID (SID) generation. We propose Agentic Generative Retrieval (AGR), a tool-augmented paradigm that interleaves SID generation with on-demand feature acquisition. Conditioned on the request and decoded SID prefix, AGR decides whether to invoke a tool, which feature to acquire, and where its observation should influence subsequent decoding, thereby steering generation toward feature-consistent SID branches. To supervise this behavior, we construct tool-augmented trajectories based on feature applicability and information gain along the SID hierarchy, and train the model through SID pretraining followed by tool-augmented fine-tuning. Experiments on an industrial dataset and three public benchmarks, together with an online A/B test, consistently demonstrate the effectiveness of AGR.</p></details> |  |
| **[FairDiff: Mitigating the Self-Reinforcing Matthew Effect in Diffusion Recommender Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36671v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While the "Matthew Effect" and filter bubbles are widely recognized outcome-level biases in recommender systems, we reveal that Diffusion Recommender Models (DRMs) uniquely compound this issue through their generative dynamics. Rather than merely inheriting data imbalances, DRMs trigger a self-reinforcing amplification of popularity bias. We identify that this phenomenon is driven by two compounding mechanisms. First, while optimization loss is universally dominated by high-frequency items across recommenders, DRMs suffer from a unique structural prior mismatch during generation. Because the forward terminal distribution of long-tailed data deviates significantly from the standard Gaussian prior, reverse sampling trajectories inherently collapse toward high-density popular items, fundamentally suppressing niche item generation. To dismantle this self-reinforcing loop, we propose FairDiff, a plug-and-play fairness-aware diffusion framework. To overcome the popularity-dominated loss, we introduce Popularity Condition Guidance (PCG). Rather than altering the training objective, PCG acts as an inference-time distributional reweighting mechanism, mathematically reshaping the score-based gradient field to penalize high-popularity regions and guide trajectories toward niche semantics. Furthermore, we design a Semantic Calibration (SC) Module to bridge the prior mismatch, aligning the forward and reverse distributions via one-step optimal transport. Comprehensive evaluations demonstrate that FairDiff achieves state-of-the-art performance while effectively mitigating the self-reinforcing Matthew Effect, highlighting its value as a general framework for DRMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Making Collaborative Signals Count: Graph-Aware Large Language Models for Sequential Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12184v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have been widely adopted as backbones for recommender systems. However, their language-centric pretraining makes it difficult to capture collaborative signals implicit in user-item interactions, which are crucial for personalized recommendation. Existing methods either inject collaborative representations produced by external recommenders or model only intra-sequence dependencies, limiting their ability to exploit global collaborative patterns. To address this limitation, we propose GALLM, a graph-aware LLM framework for sequential recommendation. GALLM constructs a collaborative graph over text tokens and item tokens, and models three types of relations: Text--Text relations for preserving semantic dependencies, Item--Text relations for aligning item tokens with their textual descriptions, and Item--Item relations derived from global item co-occurrence patterns. These relations are transformed into lightweight learnable attention biases and incorporated into the LLM attention mechanism, enabling collaborative-aware token interactions without introducing an additional graph encoder. Experiments on four real-world benchmarks show that GALLM achieves the best performance among the compared baselines, improving over the strongest baseline by 9.76\% on average in HR@5. The code is available at https://github.com/Fenglin-miku/GALLM.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables, includes appendices</p></details> |
| **[FLMNet: Self-Ensemble Distillation from Masks to Seeds for Single-Tower CTR Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26143v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Click-through rate (CTR) prediction underpins recommender systems and online advertising, where high-dimensional categorical features interact in combinatorially many ways. Several recent CTR models, such as FinalNet and QNN, report higher accuracy when a network also learns from its own averaged predictions, for example by running the same network under different dropout patterns and training each run toward the average. Why this self-distillation helps, and whether its random sampling is needed, remains unclear. This work shows that as the number of dropout patterns grows without bound, the objective reduces exactly to the label loss of the logit-averaged prediction plus a penalty on how much the dropout copies of the network disagree. When dropout does not affect how a network gates its features, this penalty can be approximated in closed form, and FLMNet is designed on this principle and trains without sampling. Networks trained from different seeds disagree in the same form, and distilling several of them into one network recovers part of their ensemble gain. On five benchmarks against seventeen baselines, the resulting models take the top two places in accuracy with a single network at inference, and the gains of the penalty and of distillation, where measurable, largely add up.</p></details> | <details><summary>44 pa...</summary><p>44 pages, 10 figures, 15 tables. Code: https://github.com/ivanna10240823-rgb/flmnet</p></details> |
| **[Decision4Rec: A Jev-Inspired Framework of Decision Models on Knowledge and Active Graphs for Multi-Agent Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26035v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Decision models, such as the commercial Jev, are language models that return one probability for each option of a question instead of generating text. We present Decision4Rec, an open and modular framework that makes decision models useful for recommendation. A knowledge graph writes the logs of each recommendation scenario as short statements on a defined scale, such as "sold: top 10%". A pool of agents, proposes candidates, and a residual ranker keeps the best 30. A decision model, trained once on real choices of three scenarios, reads them through one interface of typed questions, and a fusion step combines its answers with the score of the ranker. Any model with this interface can be used, and we run the commercial Jev, the released open Kev and Kev models trained by us on recommendation requests. The framework with a trained 4B decision model is 25 to 36% above the best trained baseline in NDCG@10 in three sparse Amazon catalogues, one of them never used to train the decision model, and almost tie with baselines on the dense Steam dataset. Most of the gain comes from the pool, and with a trained recommender it comes from what the agents know about its candidates rather than from new candidates. The 27B decision model adds to the pool 5 to 6% above the 0.8B model in three of four domains. A trained 0.8B open model beats the released Jev in eleven of fifteen settings and is never worse. On MovieLens-1M, a dense domain never seen in training, the gain is 5 to 7%. Code, configurations and trained models are available at https://github.com/marcopoli/decision4rec and https://huggingface.co/m-polignano.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multimodal Decoupling for Diversified Recommendation through Semantic and Structural Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26024v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommender systems often suffer from over-concentrated results when optimized for accuracy, limiting recommendation diversity. While diversity has become an important objective, existing methods still face fundamental limitations from both semantic and structural perspectives. On the one hand, multimodal recommender systems provide rich semantic signals, yet are often treated as auxiliary to ID embeddings, leading to ineffective semantic utilization for diversity. On the other hand, the accuracy--diversity trade-off persists during training, where accuracy gains often come at the cost of diversity. We show that this phenomenon is fundamentally governed by both the structural properties of learned representations and the dynamics of optimization. To address these challenges, we propose MOSS, a unified framework for multimodal decoupling through semantic and structural shaping. Specifically, we first introduce an MLLM-based Decoupled Feature Extraction module to disentangle multimodal content into informative representations. To enhance semantic utilization, we design a Category-aware Embedding Selection module that extracts compact, category-aware semantic features. To address structural limitations, we propose two complementary modules: Alternating Dual-Graph Propagation, which alleviates representation collapse by integrating collaborative and multimodal propagation, and Entropy-based Diversity Regularization, which mitigates optimization bias by promoting balanced category exposure. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that MOSS consistently achieves a superior accuracy--diversity trade-off over strong baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning Complementary Agent Groups via Task Hypergraphs for Next-POI Recommendation with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25801v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Next point-of-interest (POI) recommendation uses visit histories and POI content, but text and images are often missing. Large language model agents have been applied to recommender systems for preference modeling and evidence acquisition. The challenge is to select agents that can jointly analyze the available evidence for each request. We propose \textsc{CATH} (\emph{Complementary Agents via Task Hypergraphs}), which learns to select agent groups for tasks such as comparing candidates or analyzing a POI with missing content. Agents with distinct processing roles are the task hypergraph nodes; each hyperedge connects the selected group and records its analysis goal and target POIs. The policy selects members conditioned on the request, task, and agents already selected. During execution, members share candidate-linked results that guide their subsequent operations. Completed tasks update candidate states and agent memories within the request. A shared ranking head converts candidate states into corrections to a fixed base ranking. Supervised ranking trains the executor, followed by policy optimization that accounts for computation cost. Experiments under natural missingness on Gowalla-CA, Foursquare-NYC, and Foursquare-TKY show 8.4--9.1\% relative NDCG@20 gains over HyperAgent4POI, the strongest prior baseline evaluated. Additional analyses compare group-selection and execution designs, test ranking under additional masking, and characterize quality--cost tradeoffs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evidence Before Sampling: Interpretable Implicit Negative Candidate Discovery for Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07708v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommender systems learn from observed user-item interactions, but explicit negative feedback is often unavailable. Since deep learning models require negative signals for training, negative sampling methods typically treat selected unobserved interactions as negatives. However, a missing interaction does not explain why a user is uninterested in an item or whether there is sufficient evidence to label it negative. This is especially important in business recommendation, where negative signals should be interpretable and aligned with business objectives. We formulate implicit negative candidate discovery to identify unobserved interactions supported by observed customer behavior. We encode these patterns as symbolic rules, score them based on support, informativeness, and product relevance, and rank the retained rules by evidence. An LLM then interprets the retained rules using business objectives and domain knowledge; the interpretations are combined with the statistical evidence in the final report. We evaluate our method in an industrial B2B setting and across five public recommendation datasets. Candidate-quality evaluations in the industrial setting and three public datasets show higher precision than the evaluated baselines, while symbolic selection improves downstream test PR-AUC by 12.5% over random selection with four negatives per positive example in the industrial task. Our results show that negative candidate validity can be evaluated separately from downstream recommendation performance. This distinction enables evidence-based, business-aligned, and explainable negative selection, improving both interpretability and model training in sparse, skewed, real-world recommendation settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[P$^3$Rec: Distilling Prior--Posterior Preference Reasoning for LLM-based Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13993v4)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) exhibit strong semantic understanding and preference reasoning capabilities, offering new opportunities for user modeling in recommender systems. Existing LLM-as-Enhancer methods typically distill LLM-derived preference knowledge into lightweight recommenders to avoid costly online LLM inference. However, they often construct distillation knowledge from only one perspective. Prior preference captures users' stable and consistent interests but provides limited guidance for the current decision, whereas posterior preference reveals target-relevant fine-grained interests but may rely excessively on target clues. To address these limitations, we propose P$^3$Rec, a framework that jointly extracts and internalizes complementary prior and posterior preference reasoning knowledge. Specifically, P$^3$Rec first derives target-agnostic prior preferences and target-conditioned posterior preferences from the user side, while further extracting item-centric preference representations from item semantics and predecessor interactions. It then progressively internalizes prior and posterior knowledge into behavioral representations through prior preference absorption and posterior-guided preference distillation. Since the resulting comprehensive preference representation may not always provide an equally decisive retrieval direction, P$^3$Rec further characterizes historical interest dispersion with interest entropy and adaptively calibrates the user representation before contrastive retrieval optimization. In this way, P$^3$Rec achieves more complete preference reasoning while preserving efficient recommendation. Extensive experiments on multiple public datasets demonstrate its effectiveness.</p></details> | <details><summary>Due t...</summary><p>Due to unresolved disagreements among the authors regarding the content and public dissemination of this manuscript, we have decided to withdraw the paper until these issues are resolved.</p></details> |
| **[Is Serving-Time Language-Model Inference Necessary for Recommendation? Evidence from Generative and Agentic Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01260v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language models (LMs) are increasingly used in recommender systems to generate recommendations or coordinate recommendation tools. However, does their strong performance imply that serving-time LM inference is necessary? We challenge this seemingly reasonable but unverified assumption. To investigate this question, we introduce recovery-based mechanism auditing, which tests whether independent alternatives can achieve the accuracy of LM-based recommender systems without the audited inference mechanisms under controlled information and evaluation conditions. For generative recommendation, we propose LIME-Rec, which combines sequential, collaborative, and offline semantic information without autoregressive LM decoding. On three Amazon Reviews datasets (Beauty, Toys, and Sports), LIME-Rec achieves the highest reported mean in all 12 dataset-metric combinations, with up to a 15.2% relative improvement in Recall@10 over GRAM, an LM-based generative recommender. For agentic recommendation, we propose DART-Rec, which replaces LM-based tool control with deterministic fusion and fixed expert selection. On 1,000 Beauty users, given the same candidate items and expert scores, deterministic fusion achieves a 16.7% relative improvement in Recall@10 over a fine-tuned LM controller. With at most two tool calls, fixed expert selection matches the LM controllers' Hit@10 outcomes for every user in 17 of 18 comparisons. These findings demonstrate that strong recommendation performance does not necessarily require serving-time LM inference: under the evaluated task and information conditions, alternatives without the specified inference mechanisms can match or exceed the accuracy of their LM-based counterparts. To support reproducibility, code is available at https://github.com/Double-wk/LIME-Rec.</p></details> |  |
| **[Constant-Factor Local Approximation for Bipartite Densest Subgraph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24114v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given a vertex $v$ in a large bipartite graph $G$, can one quickly find a small densely connected region near $v$? This is the algorithmic core of many fraud detection, community detection, and recommender systems. One would like a local algorithm, i.e., an algorithm that probes $G$ in only a small number of locations to find such a region. Our main contribution is the first constant-factor local approximation, independent of the maximum degree of the graph, for the classical notion of bipartite density: if the sides of a bipartite subgraph are $S$ and $T$, its density is $d(S,T):=e(S,T)/\sqrt{|S||T|}$, where $e(S,T)$ is the number of edges between $S$ and $T$. We consider cohesive dense subgraphs, since every dense subgraph contains a cohesive core of at least the same density. For such a cohesive dense subgraph $S\mathbin{\dot\cup}T$ with at most $k$ vertices per side, our algorithm, starting from any vertex in $S\mathbin{\dot\cup}T$, uses $O_ε(k^{4+ε})$ neighbor queries to find a subgraph $X\mathbin{\dot\cup}Y$ of density $Ω_ε(d(S,T))$ on $O_ε(k^{2+ε})$ vertices, for every fixed $ε>0$. Our result improves upon the result of Andersen (2010), whose approximation guarantee depends logarithmically on the maximum degree. We complement our positive result with information-theoretic and computational limitations showing that the quadratic dependence on $k$ in the output size is essentially unavoidable for constant-factor approximation. Our main technical innovation is an adaptation of the Lovász-Simonovits curve to the bipartite density problem, based on weak-$\ell_p$ norm bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[LSNR: Learned Sparse News Recommendations with Attentive User Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23510v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current learned sparse approaches for recommender systems, such as STAR, represent a user by concatenating their interaction history into a single query string. For news, this creates two bottlenecks: concatenation collapses a user's distinct reading interests into one flat query, and, because news articles are long-form text, concatenating several news articles quickly overruns the context window of the language model used as backbone and forces quality-degrading truncation. To address both bottlenecks, we introduce learned sparse news recommendations (LSNR). LSNR encodes each article independently and constructs a sparse user vector by combining learned, attention-based weighting of the per-article sparse representations with a dense-projected expansion at the user level. Experiments on the MIND and EBNeRD datasets show that LSNR significantly outperforms STAR, with these gains holding even in short-history settings where truncation is not a factor. Compared to state-of-the-art dense recommenders, LSNR is competitive in standard settings and it outperforms them when the user history is short. An ablation of the user encoder confirms that learned attention-based aggregation outperforms simpler pooling alternatives. Beyond effectiveness, an additional advantage of LSNR is that it serves from an index an order of magnitude smaller than an uncompressed dense baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 3 figures, 9 tables. Accepted at SIGIR-AP 2026</p></details> |
| **[UniRec: Cross-stage Multi-Task Fusion with Preference Optimization for Cascaded Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11052v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Industrial recommender systems use cascaded stages with different candidate spaces, data distributions, and latency constraints. Independently optimizing their multi-task fusion (MTF) modules can cause cross-stage preference inconsistency, while existing cross-stage methods mainly transfer supervision to upstream scoring models and leave fusion modules jointly unoptimized. We propose UniRec, a Unified Cross-stage Recommendation Fusion framework that jointly optimizes pre-ranking and ranking fusion agents. UniRec employs a coupled dual-agent architecture with partially shared embeddings and stage-specific fusion functions. It further introduces Dual-Axis Preference Optimization to combine stage-local preferences with downstream-to-upstream preference transfer. Attribute Group-Relative Regularization (AGRR) mitigates reward-induced distribution drift by normalizing preference advantages within attribute groups. To improve efficiency, Compact Pairwise Preference Aggregation (CPPA) reduces the objective-dependent pairwise complexity, while Confidence-Aware Pair Pruning (CAPP) further reduces the evaluated pair space. Extensive offline and online experiments on a production short-video system demonstrate consistent gains over single-stage fusion and cross-stage coordination baselines. In a nine-week online A/B test, UniRec improves total watch time by $0.675\%$ and app usage time by $0.616\%$, and has been fully deployed in production.</p></details> |  |
| **[Encoded but Exposed: Language Embeddings can Reveal More Than They Should in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21713v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language embeddings are increasingly used in recommenders to represent item content, but their privacy risks remain poorly understood. We study whether item embeddings can reveal item identities when an adversary has access to auxiliary item text from external sources. We show that a simple cosine-similarity attack can recover item identities with high mean reciprocal rank across language models and candidate-set settings on two real-world datasets. Leakage is strongest when the adversary's partial information overlaps with the victim representation, but it also persists through semantic alignment between independently sourced descriptions. We further show that item leakage can propagate to user re-identification from sparse ratings. Finally, we evaluate obfuscated descriptions as a mitigation and find a clear leakage-utility tradeoff.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Quantum Machine Learning Approach to Content-Based Media Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21562v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We use quantum machine learning techniques to develop a content-based media recommender system and evaluate its performance on TV programmes using textual descriptions from one week of BBC iPlayer data. Grounded in the Quantum Natural Language Processing framework (QNLP), programme synopses are mapped to variational quantum circuits using their semantic structure. The parameters of these circuits are trained via a supervised pairwise similarity learning task. We benchmark the quantum recommender against a classical BiLSTM baseline trained on the same dataset and objective. While both models achieve comparable performance on the similarity learning task, the quantum model requires significantly fewer parameters (47k vs. 17.8M). When the BiLSTM is constrained to a matching parameter budget, its performance degrades substantially (by 3.63 percentage points in Mean Average Precision (MAP)), indicating that the structured quantum representation provides a highly parameter-efficient alternative in this setting. We demonstrate the feasibility of executing the trained models on quantum hardware by deploying the circuits on Quantinuum's System Model H2 (and accompanying emulator) using a SWAP-test-based protocol. The results confirm that the similarity signal is preserved under realistic, noisy conditions. Taken together, these findings highlight the potential of variational quantum circuits for recommendation tasks and represent a practical step toward their real-world deployment.</p></details> |  |
| **[RecSoup: Efficient Model Merging Approach for Controllable Multi-Objective Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21220v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommender systems often need to balance multiple conflicting objectives. Controllable Multi-Objective Recommendation (CMOR) enables dynamic adjustment of objective trade-offs at inference time without retraining, adapting to shifting user or system requirements. Existing CMOR approaches typically rely on output-level reranking or parameter-generating modules, which either limit the scope of feasible trade-offs or incur substantial training and inference cost. Model merging provides a promising alternative by reusing multiple objective-specific models via parameter combination. However, its direct application to recommender systems is challenging due to the lack of a shared linear regime and cross-objective interference. To address these issues, we propose RecSoup, a lightweight two-stage approach for effective CMOR via specialized model merging. RecSoup first mitigates parameter interference across objectives through a pruning strategy that jointly considers objective-specific importance and cross-objective relevance. It then performs adaptive parameter merging via a learnable preference-to-coefficient mapping, allowing dynamic trade-off control at inference time without joint retraining or additional generative modules. Theoretically, we prove that RecSoup outperforms the parameter generation paradigm in effectiveness and efficiency, as it can fully navigate the Pareto front without smoothness constraints. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that RecSoup achieves improved Pareto front quality and effective controllability while delivers 165--228x faster inference-time latency than parameter generation approaches. Code is available at https://github.com/35798642/RECSOUP.git.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CIKM 2026</p></details> |
| **[From Clicks to Intent: Cross-Platform Session Embeddings with LLM-Distilled Taxonomy for Financial Services Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26277v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sequential user behavior modeling is widely adopted in industrial recommender systems; however, significant gaps remain in financial services, where pre-login web interactions and authenticated in-app experiences differ drastically. Specifically, pre-login web users typically explore new products, whereas logged-in app users focus on account servicing. Due to the challenge of cross-channel entity resolution (e.g., matching anonymous web sessions to authenticated mobile accounts), web-based intent signals remain underutilized for post-authentication personalization. Existing methods for capturing web-based intent are often ad-hoc and narrow, lacking the flexibility to support both quantitative downstream recommendations and qualitative understanding at scale. In this work, we propose a scalable and dual-purpose intent prediction framework for web-based interactions and demonstrate its applicability for personalization. Our approach transforms raw web clickstreams into two outputs: a self-supervised Transformer encodes multi-modal clickstreams into a compact session embedding, while an LLM-based taxonomy generation and distillation pipeline produces interpretable intent labels. Our system demonstrates that self-supervised clickstream representations combined with LLM-distilled taxonomies can jointly serve quantitative tasks and qualitative understanding in production: on the mobile homepage tile ranking task, the session embedding improves macro Recall@1 by 1.88% and reduces Log Loss by 13.38% over production baselines. On the user conversion prediction task, the embedding outperforms the LLM labels by 4.3% on micro F1, while the distillation layer delivers interpretable labels at ultra-low latency with only a 7% performance drop.</p></details> | <details><summary>Dianj...</summary><p>Dianjing Fan and Yao Li equally contributed to this work. 7 pages, 1 figure</p></details> |
| **[On the Impact of Graph Neural Networks in Recommender Systems: A Topological Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.07384v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recommender systems, user-item interactions can be modeled as a bipartite graph, where user and item nodes are connected by undirected edges. This graph-based view has motivated the rapid adoption of graph neural networks (GNNs), which often outperform collaborative filtering (CF) methods such as latent factor models, deep neural networks, and generative strategies. Yet, despite their empirical success, the reasons why GNNs offer systematic advantages over other CF approaches remain only partially understood. This monograph advances a topology-centered perspective on GNN-based recommendation. We argue that a comprehensive understanding of these models' performance should consider the structural properties of user-item graphs and their interaction with GNN architectural design. To support this view, we introduce a formal taxonomy that distills common modeling patterns across eleven representative GNN-based recommendation approaches and consolidates them into a unified conceptual pipeline. We further formalize thirteen classical and topological characteristics of recommendation datasets and reinterpret them through the lens of graph machine learning. Using these definitions, we analyze the considered GNN-based recommender architectures to assess how and to what extent they encode such properties. Building on this analysis, we derive an explanatory framework that links measurable dataset characteristics to model behavior and performance. Taken together, this monograph re-frames GNN-based recommendation through its topological underpinnings and outlines open theoretical, data-centric, and evaluation challenges for the next generation of topology-aware recommender systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in Foundations and Trends in Information Retrieval</p></details> |
| **[Bridging the Topological Divide: Dual-Branch Graph Distillation with Semantic Priors for Robust Cold-Start Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19578v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph Collaborative Filtering models have achieved remarkable success in recommendation systems. However, they inherently suffer from a catastrophic representation collapse in strict cold-start scenarios due to the topological isolation of unobserved items. While integrating multi-modal semantic features offers a potential remedy, directly mapping isolated semantic attributes into a globally topology-driven collaborative space frequently precipitates representation degradation and optimization conflicts. To bridge this fundamental structural divide, we propose DualGCN, an innovative Dual-Branch Graph Knowledge Distillation Framework. Operating under a decoupled two-stage training paradigm, DualGCN first constructs a robust collaborative teacher network where item representations are anchored by a topology-smoothed semantic prior, effectively reshaping the latent manifold. Subsequently, a semantic student branch employs a residual mapping mechanism and a magnitude-agnostic cosine distillation objective to achieve high-fidelity cross-modal alignment without disrupting established collaborative signals. Furthermore, we introduce a dynamic norm reconstruction mechanism to rigorously calibrate the physical scales of novel and warm items during global retrieval. Extensive experiments across three real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that DualGCN significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in strict cold-start scenarios while comprehensively preserving recommendation accuracy for highly active items.</p></details> |  |
| **[xLightGCN: Explainable Graph Neural Network Based Recommendation Systems with Most Influential Peers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19446v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommendation systems, systems that predict a user's preference for items in order to curate that user's feed, are a ubiquitous and central feature in online social networks. While they are a central piece of our modern digital infrastructure, many modern approaches are opaque black boxes, making them difficult to audit, understand, and assess the trustworthiness of. In this paper we present an exact, graph-based approach for generating explanations for decisions made by recommendation systems. We argue that in the risk-sensitive sociotechnical context of user-facing AI such as recommendation systems, explainability is a key pillar for establishing the trustworthiness of models and validating their effectiveness. We propose a technique built on LightGCN like architectures, an influential and popular class of recommendation models. We describe our approach and argue that it provides an appropriate assurance mechanism for these AI systems leading to specific advantages, including interpretable explanations and a theoretically guaranteed measure of explanation fidelity. We conclude by discussing limitations and directions for future research.</p></details> |  |
| **[Prompt or Policy? Optimizing Physician Slate Presentations with LLM User Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18309v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Choosing the right physician is a key challenge for patients seeking medical care. Physician recommendation systems identify relevant doctors, but patients still find it difficult to compare their profiles to make a decision. We formulate slate presentation optimization: given a patient's medical case and a fixed, ranked list of physicians, we generate short, case-specific summaries that make doctors easier to compare, while remaining grounded in the information in their profiles. To optimize physician presentations without relying on real patient interactions, we develop an offline framework that uses persona-conditioned LLMs to simulate patient choices. We generate patient personas from attributes extracted from each medical case and evaluate presentations for factual accuracy, relevance, naturalness and simulated patient choice. We also use a second LLM judge, unseen during optimization, to assess simulator-specific overfitting. We compare automatic prompt-level optimization with GEPA, parameter-level optimization with GRPO, and their combination across two model sizes. GRPO substantially outperforms GEPA in satisfying the full set of constraints, and its gains in simulated conversion persist under the unseen judge. GEPA alone brings smaller improvements, but using its optimized prompt to initialize GRPO speeds up training and achieves the highest simulated conversion. These results show that prompt-level and parameter-level optimization play complementary roles in improving how physician recommendations are presented.</p></details> |  |
| **[SimInter: Evidence-Grounded User Simulation with Interview-Based Profile Enrichment for Conversational Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18308v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational Recommender Systems (CRSs) require reliable user simulators for cost-effective evaluation. | **[Web Scale on a Budget: Tail-Aware Retrieval Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17986v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A common practice in instance retrieval is to grow the gallery size to improve a benchmark's discrimination capability. One prominent example is ILIAS, which adds up to 100 million distractor images. The prevailing assumption is that faithful evaluation demands web scale, and that indexing all 100M images through the backbone is the unavoidable price of a comparable number. That price is steep: hundreds of GPU-hours and ~200 GB of descriptors per model, paid again for every method or ablation. We show the web-scale indexing price is largely avoidable: evaluation itself obeys a scaling law. The number of distractors that outrank a positive is Binomial in the gallery size. We define the per-query retrieval hardness, a gallery-scale agnostic quantity that yields a retrieval metric at any scale, and demonstrate it can be derived from a small random sample. The underlying sampled-rank machinery is long established in recommender-system and biometric evaluation, but to our knowledge has never been adapted to visual retrieval. We make the law per-query and estimable, and validate it at true 100M scale on ILIAS and INSTRE, and on the full 759k-distractor Google Landmarks v2 pool. One measure of the (in)stability of the outcome is the number of voters who prefer an unchosen alternative to all alternatives in the committee. If this number is less than half the electorate, the committee is a Condorcet winning set, which may not exist if $k \leq 2$. More generally, there exist instances where any committee of size $k$ has at least a $\frac{2}{(k+1)}-ε$ fraction of the electorate preferring another alternative, for arbitrarily small $ε$. While recent work has made remarkable progress narrowing the gap, it is currently unknown whether this lower bound is tight. We develop a mixed-integer linear programming framework that computes exact bounds for fixed parameter values. Our approach depends only on $m$ and $k$; by modeling electorates as distributions over rankings, we eliminate any dependence on $n$. Our programs allow us to rule out counterexamples over arbitrary electorate sizes and obtain tight bounds for fixed $m$ and $k$. Further, our framework provides a pathway for proving general upper bounds. In particular, we show a linear regime, $k \ge \frac{8m}{9}$, in which the conjectured $\frac{2}{(k+1)}$ upper bound indeed holds, providing the first results that match known lower bounds. | **[Technical Report on the Turba Fertilizer Machine Learning Stack in Morocco](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05949v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Site-specific fertilizer recommendation systems adapt nutrient advice to location, soil properties, crop type, and production targets, but scientific reuse is constrained when recommendation functions remain accessible mainly through interactive interfaces, outputs are not versioned, and trained approximations cannot be independently loaded or benchmarked. However, a key challenge in EDTs is the enormous space of possible actions that one can take at each step: users struggle to choose among thousands of joins, transformations, and aggregations, causing "exploration paralysis". Because EDT workflows are interconnected, each choice impacts subsequent exploration, and suboptimal choices can lead to inefficiency, missed insights, confirmation bias, and incomplete coverage. This calls for intelligent recommendations that efficiently guide users toward optimal EDT actions. We envision recommendation as a core capability of data systems, proactively guiding users toward promising actions and thereby lowering the barrier to exploratory data tasks. We outline a research agenda that progresses from recommending individual EDT actions to constrained bundles and sequences of actions, and ultimately to coordinated recommendations across interconnected EDTs. | **[Towards More Efficient, Robust, Instance-adaptive, and Generalizable Sequential Decision making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09192v5)** | 2026-09-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The primary goal of my Ph.D. study is to develop provably efficient and practical algorithms for data-driven sequential decision-making under uncertainty. My work focuses on reinforcement learning (RL), multi-armed bandits, and their applications, including recommendation systems, computer networks, video analytics, and large language models (LLMs). Sequential decision-making methods, such as bandits and RL, have demonstrated remarkable success - ranging from outperforming human players in complex games like Atari and Go to advancing robotics, recommendation systems, and fine-tuning LLMs. Despite these successes, many established algorithms rely on idealized models that can fail under model misspecifications or adversarial perturbations, particularly in settings where accurate prior knowledge of the underlying model class is unavailable or where malicious users operate within dynamic systems. These challenges are pervasive in real-world applications, where robust and adaptive solutions are critical. Furthermore, while worst-case guarantees provide theoretical reliability, they often fail to capture instance-dependent performance, which can lead to more efficient and practical solutions. Another key challenge lies in generalizing to new, unseen environments, a crucial requirement for deploying these methods in dynamic and unpredictable settings. To address these limitations, my research aims to develop more efficient, robust, instance-adaptive, and generalizable sequential decision-making algorithms for both reinforcement learning and bandits. Towards this end, I focus on developing more efficient, robust, instance-adaptive, and generalizable for both general reinforcement learning (RL) and bandits.</p></details> | Ph.D. Thesis |

Then building on these findings, we propose VirusCascade, the first black-box targeted promotion attack that jointly shapes semantic and structural attack surfaces: the former ensures the target item is naturally rationalised as satisfying broad user preferences, the latter positions it for system-wide propagation. ## Representation Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[SpecMRL: Redesign Matryoshka Representation Learning within Parametric Spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26824v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) is designed to support flexible representation sizes by jointly optimizing nested prefixes. While effective, this design lacks an explicit constraint to ensure that the leading portion provides a suitable compact encoding. To address this, we propose SpecMRL, which reorganizes the feature space to enable more effective prefix readout. Specifically, we extend MRL with a parameter-derived spectral basis organized with feature importance. SpecMRL otherwise follows standard MRL, with prefix readout performed in the learned spectral space. Experiments on MTEB show that SpecMRL outperforms MRL at all evaluated reduced dimensions, with larger gains under more aggressive compression. These results suggest that the learned spectral basis better prioritizes informative directions, enabling more effective representation compression. Notably, SpecMRL extends the performance frontier of MRL, surpassing full-dimensional MRL with only half the embedding dimensions.</p></details> | preprint |
| **[TriFold: Folding irregular 3D Gaussians into structured latents for 3D Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23050v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent progress in 3D generation has motivated growing interest in compact generative representations of complex 3D scenes. For native 3D Gaussian scenes, however, learning such representations remains challenging due to widely varying primitive counts, nonuniform spatial density, and differences in geometry and appearance even among spatially neighboring primitives. In this work, we introduce TriFold, a native 3D Gaussian VAE that encodes Gaussian scenes into a fixed-layout triplane latent and jointly reconstructs their geometry and appearance. The key insight is to impose structural regularity on the latent space while preserving the spatial adaptivity of the underlying Gaussians. During encoding, we use cell-wise learned queries to aggregate projected Gaussians into multiple slots per cell, reducing the information loss caused by single-feature pooling. During decoding, occupancy prediction identifies active regions, where query-based set prediction and candidate selection reconstruct local Gaussian sets with spatially varying primitive counts. Building on this representation, we train a latent flow-matching model for conditional scene-level 3DGS generation, enabling point-cloud-conditioned synthesis and single-image-conditioned amodal generation. Experiments demonstrate that TriFold achieves high-fidelity reconstruction and generation from a single image that provides only a partial observation of the scene, validating its effectiveness for native 3D Gaussian representation learning. https://fantasy-amap.github.io/TriFold</p></details> | <details><summary>30pag...</summary><p>30pages, Project page: https://fantasy-amap.github.io/TriFold</p></details> |
| **[Hierarchical Token Merging with Recursive Autoencoders for Unsupervised Grammar Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26695v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hierarchical abstraction is a fundamental principle across many data modalities, enabling complex sequences to be represented compositionally at multiple levels. While autoencoders learn compressed representations through reconstruction, this objective does not necessarily favour meaningful compositional structure. We study this challenge through unsupervised grammar induction, where the underlying hierarchy can be evaluated explicitly. | **[Global Time-Aware Conditioning (GTC): A Mechanism for Time-Aware Pediatric Sleep Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26618v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sleep physiology evolves throughout the night, with changes in sleep cycles, respiratory stability, and cardiovascular dynamics. | **[STAR: Adaptive Spatial-Temporal Normalization for Unified Microservice Incident Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31645v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated incident management in large-scale microservice systems relies on learning robust representations from multimodal observability data, including metrics, logs, and traces. In this paper, we propose STAR, a Spatial-Temporal Adaptive Representation learning framework that explicitly addresses these challenges through adaptive normalizations. STAR introduces two tightly coupled mechanisms: Temporal Adaptive Normalization (TAN), which dynamically normalizes multivariate time series using multi-scale temporal context, and Spatial Adaptive Normalization (SAN), which performs structure-aware normalization over service dependency graphs. Unlike prior methods that treat normalization as static or task-agnostic, STAR formulates it as a learnable, context-conditioned transformation aligned with the intrinsic properties of microservice systems. The resulting adaptive representations are integrated into a unified self-supervised framework, enabling end-to-end unsupervised support for AD, FT, and RCL tasks. Extensive experiments on two real-world microservice benchmarks demonstrate that STAR consistently outperforms all state-of-the-art baselines, yielding significant and stable improvements across all three tasks. | **[AirMPA: A Meteorology-to-Pollution Adapter for Global Air Quality Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25797v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate real-time forecasting of atmospheric pollutants is essential for reducing exposure risks and improving public health. | **[DeReCo: Decoupling Representation and Coordination Learning for Object-Adaptive Decentralized Multi-Robot Cooperative Grasping and Lifting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08111v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalizing decentralized multi-robot cooperative grasping and lifting across objects with diverse shapes and physical properties remains a fundamental challenge. | **[From Prediction to Action: Population Health World Model for Digital Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24662v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Digital medicine now captures data that traces individual health trajectories and reveals population health dynamics through electronic health records, claims, remote monitoring, wearable devices, mobile devices, social sensing data, medical images, etc. But much of its modeling still centers on task-specific, outcome-specific, and prediction-specific estimation. The next frontier is not simply better prediction, but action in an evolving health world shaped by multiple decision-makers, heterogeneous populations, resource constraints, and feedback in care delivery. This is why digital medicine now needs world models: not only to estimate what will happen, but to represent how actions change what can happen next -- whom to prioritize, how to allocate scarce resources, how to adapt care across subgroups, and how interventions reshape future health trajectories and population dynamics. We call such a model population health world model (PHWM): a more foundational representation of an evolving health world in which health states, individual trajectories, population health dynamics, interventions, constraints, and feedback are intrinsically coupled. Regulation also supports learning through evaluation, trusted data access, interoperability and safety standards. The argument is that sovereignty depends on converting distributed resources and knowledge into usable, coordinated and responsibly evaluated capability. Yet interventions are defined in the mathematical model, whereas the resulting model predictions concern the effects of actions in the real world. Within this framework, we prove an impossibility result: no interpretation can simultaneously satisfy a collection of desirable properties while remaining non-circular. Under a circular interpretation, every model that fits the observational distribution is interventionally valid, so no experiment can falsify it. We analyze several non-circular alternatives and discuss implications for falsification, causal discovery, causal representation learning, and causal abstraction. However, identifiability does not imply interpretability: latent semantics are typically assigned post hoc by alignment with known ground-truth factors. In contrast, scientific observations (such as residue-pair distances, climate indices, or process sensors) are inherently semantic, as they correspond to named physical quantities. MOSAIC identifies latent variables via regime-conditioned temporal variation, and recovers for each latent a sparse set of associated observations through an additive decoder, yielding module-level interpretability. We show that ANOVA main-effect supports are identifiable under general smooth mixing functions, with finite-sample recovery guarantees for a group-lasso estimator and empirical evaluation of MOSAIC's entropy regularizer. Empirically, MOSAIC recovers domain-consistent variable groups across RNA molecular dynamics, solar wind, ENSO climate, the Tennessee Eastman process, and a synthetic tokamak benchmark, enabling interpretable discovery of latent mechanisms in scientific time series.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 39 pages, including references and appendices</p></details> |
| **[FlexUMM: Flexible Representation Learning for Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22682v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Unified multimodal models (UMMs) can integrate understanding and generation within a single backbone. We evaluate CUL on a 589-scan 3D prostate MRI multi-structure segmentation task (8 anatomical structures) using a ViT with UperNet decoder architecture. Average Dice ($83.38\%$) remains comparable to the baseline ($83.35\%$), while surface distance decreases significantly ($p<0.001$) across 6 of 8 anatomical structures. A hard-case analysis reveals a $22{\times}$ asymmetry between significantly improved and degraded cases ($18.8\%$ vs $0.9\%$). Ablation studies show that removing uncertainty-guided sample selection eliminates improvements for the transition zone, while replacing the contrastive objective with a boundary loss additionally removes bladder improvements, demonstrating that both uncertainty-guided sampling and the contrastive objective are essential for the observed gains. These findings demonstrate that the proposed uncertainty-guided representation learning is a useful mechanism for reducing the risk of extreme errors in safety-critical medical segmentation</p></details> | UNSURE MICCAI 2026 |

These results show that discriminative training leaves a generative functional signal for synthesis and calibration. | **[Platonic Projection Structures: Operator-Induced Observability in Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05175v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We characterize observability in representation learning through Platonic Projection Structures (PPS), an operator-theoretic framework for analyzing representation accessibility under partial observation. Rather than treating observable outputs as direct reflections of latent representations, PPS models observation through a self-adjoint positive semidefinite operator acting on a latent representation space. We show that quantum measurement and representation inference under linear observation models share this operator-theoretic structure while differing in the algebraic properties of their observation operators; the correspondence is structural rather than physical. Representation transfer and knowledge distillation can likewise be interpreted as approximate preservation of observable geometry through $ΦΠ_T \approx Π_S Φ$. PPS also reveals a structural limitation of output-based interpretability: latent components in $\ker(Π)$ are inaccessible from induced observables, imposing intrinsic constraints on attribution and explanation methods. Controlled empirical validations demonstrate kernel-invariant observability, projection-induced attribution gaps, and rank-controlled observable geometry in latent representation spaces. PPS thus provides an explicit characterization of observability through operator-induced quotient geometry and a unified perspective on representation accessibility, interpretability, and projection-mediated inference.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 7 figures. ## Graph Transformers
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Spectral Adaptive Graph Transformer for Efficient and Scalable Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25140v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic forecasting models based on spectral graph convolution offer an interpretable account of how information is mixed between sensors, but they rely on an eigendecomposition of the graph Laplacian. It costs cubic time, is fixed by the road network, and provides a symmetric operator that cannot represent the directed propagation of traffic congestion. Attention-based spatio-temporal transformers remove these limitations but generate a dense sensor-by-sensor attention map at each layer and time step, so memory, rather than accuracy, becomes the binding constraint on large networks. We introduce the spectral adaptive graph transformer, whose spatial operator is learned rather than derived from the Laplacian, directed rather than symmetric, and conditioned on the current traffic state rather than a static state, at a cost linear in the number of sensors. A sparse road graph diffusion branch provides the strictly local propagation that a low-rank operator cannot express, and a zero-initialized gate learns the balance between the two. The orbit count bounds the number of distinct values, and regular and vertex-transitive graphs admit closed forms. Second, on entropy. This raises the question of what exactly this shared memory does in this static setup. We give preliminary evidence that optimizing a shared memory directly performs similarly to global communication methods, and so normal local message-passing models can embed similar information in their weights. Thus, these settings may be a poor fit for evaluating global communication in graph neural networks.</p></details> |  |

| **[Undecidability of Confluence for Binary Length-Reducing Cycle Rewriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18859v1)** | 2026-08-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Confluence guarantees that diverging rewrite choices can always be rejoined. Confluence of finite cycle-rewriting systems over the fixed alphabet $\{0,1\}$ is undecidable, indeed $Π^0_1$-complete, even when every rule has a nonempty right-hand side and strictly reduces length. Under this restriction termination is syntactically evident, and derivations from a nonempty length-$n$ cycle have fewer than $n$ steps. These findings demonstrate the potential of SAGE as a scalable framework for socially-aware multi-agent navigation in complex environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 5 figures, and 14 tables. Includes supplementary experimental details</p></details> |
| **[Geometry-Guided Generative Representation for Functional Brain Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04539v2)** | 2026-07-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In network neuroscience, functional brain systems are often characterized using separate yet related graph-theoretic or spectral descriptors, overlooking how these properties covary and partially overlap across individuals and conditions. We anticipate that dense, weighted functional connectivity graphs lie on a low-dimensional latent geometry along which both topological and spectral structures vary smoothly at the population level. Although graph-based deep learning offers a powerful framework for modeling these brain connectomes, supervised approaches are constrained by the limited availability of labeled data. Existing unsupervised graph representation methods also typically focus on node-level embeddings, which are limited in capturing compact graph-level representations that preserve information from dense functional connectomes. To address these gaps, we learn compact brain graph representations using a graph transformer autoencoder, where domain-specific, aligned functional gradient geometry provides an inductive bias to guide learning. Despite being trained in a fully unsupervised manner, our approach meaningfully separates cognitive states and enables decoding of visual stimuli, with performance further improved by incorporating neural dynamics. In parallel, to enable generation of synthetic brain graphs, we fit a diffusion model to the learned latent representation and decode samples back to dense connectomes.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 43rd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Local equivalence of stabilizer states: a graphical characterisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.20183v4)** | 2026-07-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Stabilizer states form a ubiquitous family of quantum states that can be graphically represented through the graph state formalism. A fundamental property of graph states is that applying a local complementation - a well-known and extensively studied graph transformation - results in a graph that represents the same entanglement as the original. In other words, the corresponding graph states are LU-equivalent. This property served as the cornerstone for capturing non-trivial quantum properties in a simple graphical manner, in the study of quantum entanglement but also for developing protocols and models based on graph states and stabilizer states, such as measurement-based quantum computing, secret sharing, error correction, entanglement distribution... However, local complementation fails short to fully characterise entanglement: there exist pairs of graph states that are LU-equivalent but cannot be transformed one into the other using local complementations. Only few is known about the equivalence of graph states beyond local complementation. We introduce a generalisation of local complementation which graphically characterises the LU-equivalence of graph states. We use this characterisation to show the existence of a strict infinite hierarchy of equivalences of graph states. Our approach is based on minimal local sets, which are subsets of vertices that are known to cover any graph, and to be invariant under local complementation and even LU-equivalence. We use these structures to provide a type to each vertex of a graph, leading to a natural standard form in which the LU-equivalence can be exhibited and captured by means of generalised local complementation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Adaptive Multi-Expert Graph Transformer for Interpretable EEG-Based Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19429v1)** | 2026-07-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Electroencephalographic (EEG) abnormalities arise from dynamic changes in neural synchrony across spatial and temporal scales, yet many computational approaches reduce these dynamics to static features. We present a Spatial Multi-Expert Graph Transformer that models each EEG recording as a sequence of dynamic functional connectivity graphs. Time-resolved connectivity is estimated using the weighted Phase Lag Index (wPLI), and hierarchical graph encoding aggregates information from electrode to regional and global levels. A multi-expert transformer architecture enables subtype-aware reasoning, with a gating mechanism adaptively fusing expert outputs for global abnormality prediction. Experiments on the TUAB dataset show competitive abnormal EEG detection performance and demonstrate the potential of dynamic graph modeling with adaptive expert fusion for interpretable, subtype-aware spatial--temporal analysis.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Weisfeiler-Leman Characterization of Global-Attention Graph Transformers for Mixed-Integer Linear Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17570v1)** | 2026-07-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph foundation models (GFMs) with global attention are increasingly used to represent mixed-integer linear programs (MILPs), aiming to capture structure beyond the locality of standard graph neural networks. We study their expressive power through graph isomorphism testing, asking which MILP instances they map to identical representations. We prove that a broad class of hierarchical graph transformers combining global linear attention, edge-weighted cross-attention, and bipartite message passing is bounded by the one-dimensional Weisfeiler-Leman (1-WL) test: under any parameter setting, 1-WL-equivalent MILP graphs receive identical graph embeddings. Our compositional proof shows that each architectural component is a symmetric multiset function and thus preserves 1-WL equivalence. We validate this characterization across ten diverse graph encoders, including Graphormer-, GraphGPS-, Set-Transformer-, and Gasse-style models. Across model capacities, graph scales, and pooling operators, every tested encoder maps 1-WL-equivalent non-isomorphic graph pairs to numerically identical embeddings. Consequently, graph invariants that vary within a 1-WL equivalence class cannot be recovered from these representations. We further show that expressiveness beyond 1-WL arises from input encoding rather than attention: random-walk positional encodings separate the constructed pairs, while additional constructions expose the limits of this remedy. These results characterize the expressive power of global-attention GFMs and provide an encoder-agnostic diagnostic for detecting 1-WL-induced representation equivalence.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Topology, Algebra, and Geometry in Data Science (TAG-DS) 2026 (20 pages, 10 figures) [Lightning Oral Presentation]</p></details> |

## LLM
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26836v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.</p></details> | 15 pages, 4 figures |
| **[Safety Mid-Training: Internalizing LLM Safety as a Foundational Capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26809v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safety alignment for large language models (LLMs) is primarily performed during post-training, while recent work has also begun incorporating safety data into pre-training. Yet safety can degrade when later training no longer includes safety data, and gains in safety often come at the cost of general capability. This tension suggests that safety and general reasoning should not be treated as separate training goals. We instead view safety as an LLM capability that can be learned through a reasoning structure shared with general tasks. Both require the model to understand intent or task goals, apply relevant knowledge, and reason about potential consequences. We introduce safety mid-training, a stage between pre-training and post-training that jointly develops safety and general reasoning through this shared structure. Given safety and utility prompts, the base model generates structured documents and reasoning traces that are then used for mid-training. Safety mid-training reduces average attack success rate (ASR) by 72% to 81% relative to Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B base models, while also improving general capability. The benefits persist through subsequent post-training. After general-only SFT, mid-trained models largely retain their safety, achieving lower ASR than the baselines without sacrificing general capability. They also retain safety under general-only GRPO without safety rewards, while subsequent RL reaches a better safety-utility frontier when initialized from the mid-trained models. Together, these results suggest that learning safety and general reasoning within a shared mid-training stage can reduce the safety-utility trade-off and make safety more robust to subsequent training.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLM-Flock: LLM-Driven Decentralized Multi-Robot Flocking via Influence-Based Consensus with Collision Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06513v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in reasoning and planning, prompting researchers to explore their use as decentralized decision-makers for multi-robot formation control. However, directly applying LLMs to such tasks often leads to unstable behavior, in which robots may collapse to the centroid of their positions or diverge entirely, due to hallucinated reasoning and limited coordination awareness. We propose LLM-Flock, a decentralized multi-robot flocking framework that addresses these challenges through two key mechanisms: (1) an influence-based consensus protocol, where robots iteratively refine individually generated formation plans by adopting those of more connected neighbors, driving the team toward a unified plan without centralized control, and (2) a two-layer collision avoidance strategy that pairs LLM-based urgency reasoning with a programmatic collision checker and re-prompting mechanism. We evaluate LLM-Flock across five state-of-the-art LLMs, including both closed-source (GPT-5, Claude 4.5) and open-source (Llama3.1-405b, Qwen-Max, DeepSeek-R1) models, demonstrating notable improvements in formation stability and convergence over prior LLM-based methods. Real-world experiments with Crazyflie drones further validate the framework's applicability to physical multi-robot systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Game of Phones: Harnessing Game Theory and LLMs in 'Bot Wars' to Counteract Phone Scams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26781v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Phone scams impose substantial and growing costs on individuals and financial systems worldwide, yet existing countermeasures impose no resource cost on perpetrators. Strategic scam-baiting, in which automated agents engage scammers to deplete their operational capacity, offers a proactive alternative but requires dynamic strategy adaptation that static systems cannot provide. We introduce a game-theoretic framework enabling LLMs to learn effective counter-strategies through in-context experience without fine-tuning. The framework models scammer-baiter interactions as a two-player game with empirically grounded strategies and utility functions, using Nash equilibrium as a formal benchmark for evaluating learned behaviour. Across 300 simulated dialogues and three LLM architectures, adaptive strategy selection achieves 28.9-79.7% closer alignment to Nash equilibrium than non-adaptive baselines. The code and an annotated dataset of 300 dialogues with turn-level strategy labels and utility scores are released at https://github.com/nardinebasta/GameofPhones_/ .</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 2026 Joint European Conference on Machine Learning and Knowledge Discovery in Databases (ECML PKDD 2026)</p></details> |
| **[LLM-Driven Autonomous Vehicles Can Inherit Human Driver Biases in Pedestrian Yielding: Results and Implications From a New Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00192v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Public trust in Autonomous Vehicles (AVs) may depend not only on technical success but also on the fairness of their decision making. Given that psychology studies have shown human driver biases exist, such as lower pedestrian-yielding rates to Black pedestrians in the US, we argue that analyses of model bias should also be part of AV evaluation. Concretely, in this paper we propose two new bias testing methodologies for Large Language Models (LLMs) and Visual-Language Models (VLMs), "All Else Being Equal" tests and "Self-Consistency" tests, and a new benchmark (FairYield) to assess bias in pedestrian-yielding decisions. Our findings show that the yielding decisions of both LLMs and VLMs can be influenced by models' predictions of pedestrian gender, ethnicity, religion, disability, age, skin tone, and socio-economic status. While the type and degree of bias is different from model to model, we highlight common patterns, and raise questions about the "common sense" model paradigm, particularly the need to either revise the paradigm or address issues of downstream bias.</p></details> |  |
| **[Which Model for Which Role? Cost-Efficient Pareto Recommendation for LLM Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26676v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agentic workflows increasingly employ different models for specialized roles. Users may prefer different trade-offs between task quality and deployment cost, sometimes also considering latency. Finding good assignments incurs a separate search cost from evaluating multiple candidates. We introduce Cost-Coupled Gittins (CC-Gittins), a multi-objective extension of single-objective cost-aware Gittins search for Pareto recommendation. It exploits the coupling between evaluation and deployment costs: assignments that cost more to deploy also cost more to evaluate. The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. We evaluate CC-Gittins on eight quality--cost matrices from workflows with two to five roles and up to 1,681 candidate assignments. We also evaluate the method with latency as a third objective. Across the eight benchmarks, CC-Gittins recovers a substantial fraction of the true Pareto frontier at roughly 10% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost, and recovers most of the frontier with few false positives by 30%.</p></details> | <details><summary>Preli...</summary><p>Preliminary version accepted at NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Resource-Aware Agentic AI (RAAAI)</p></details> |
| **[AllPath: Layered Verification for a Deployed, Self-Revising LLM Trading Agent under Human-Gated and Autonomous Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26662v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM trading agents are evaluated almost exclusively in backtests and autonomous benchmark harnesses. Deployment, where actions are irreversible, raises a different question: who verifies the agent's actions, and which failures can each verifier structurally catch? We report on AllPath, a personal LLM trading agent deployed against a live paper-brokerage account. The system watches positions through a deterministic rules engine, and each night it reviews its own day and proposes revisions to its own trading strategies. Verification is split across three layers. Code verifies what must not depend on judgment: deterministic gates enforce risk limits and block any strategy revision that fails an integrity check. The agent verifies itself within fixed bounds: its nightly review has an iteration cap and no tools that can act. A human verifies judgment, through approval screens that show the current state of each proposal. We also compared two verification policies for the agent's self-revisions on the live system: two and a half weeks with every self-revision human-gated, then five weeks with reflection revisions auto-applied behind the same code gates, while the operator's chat channel stayed human-gated throughout. Each layer caught a different kind of failure. The code gates rejected no proposal; their role was keeping revisions consistent. The human rejected two proposals that passed every code check, both over a disagreement about the market, and later removed 11 of the 19 rules the agent had added on its own, at a median lag of eight days. The most instructive production incidents, such as strategies silently left unmonitored, passed every gate; the operator found them by hand, and the fix each time was an interface change that makes the condition visible. We contribute the layered architecture, the two-regime account, and a production incident taxonomy, none observable in a backtest.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 3rd Workshop on LLMs and Generative AI in Finance (AI4F) at ACM ICAIF 2026 (non-archival). 11 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables. | **[How Do Agentic LLMs Decide to Call Tools? A Tool-Call Vector Shaped by Suppression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09624v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool calling, invoking external tools on demand, is central to agentic LLMs, yet the mechanism that decides whether a model calls a tool or responds directly remains poorly understood. | **[FOXGLOVE: Understanding Goal-Oriented and Anchored Writing Feedback from Experts and LLMs on Argumentative Essays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06271v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate writing feedback, there remains no systematic comparison of LLM and expert feedback on the dimensions that writing research identifies as central to revision: goal-orientation, anchoring to specific sentences, and prioritization. | **[Small Average Error, Wide Individual Error: General-Purpose LLMs Estimating Energy Intake from Meal Photos in a Feeding Trial](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26538v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Photo-based estimation of food energy could ease dietary assessment, and general-purpose multimodal large language models (LLMs) make such estimates easy to obtain.
