Three results ran against intuition. Subtraction under-estimated intake for five of six models, because the models still report hundreds of kilocalories for a nearly empty tray. | **[REATS: LLM Reasoning-based Ensemble Learning for Adaptive Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10149v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Due to the diversity of real-world time series, no single forecasting model consistently dominates across all samples. | **[Move by Move: Measuring and Steering How LLMs Conduct Psychotherapy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21325v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As the demand for mental health services outpaces clinical workforce capacity, users are increasingly turning to large language models (LLMs) for accessible and private support. | **[RouterWorld: Scaling Conversational AI with Latency-Aware LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26511v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational AI systems built on Large Language Models (LLMs) require routing mechanisms that balance answer quality and response latency at scale. However, existing LLM routing benchmarks assume full observability of all candidate model outcomes, under-emphasize latency as a first-class metric, and fragment evaluation across heterogeneous protocols misaligned with real-world settings. Evaluating 13 routing baselines, we find that most routers over-emphasize accuracy while ignoring latency under partial observability. Code and benchmark data are publicly available.</p></details> | 28 pages, pre-print |

By deriving both representations directly during prefill, ESTP captures uncertainty and semantic importance while incurring negligible additional inference latency and memory overhead compared with methods using external auxiliary models. We further find that interaction behaviors provide evidence for these strategies, which can be decomposed and recombined for reuse across tasks. Promptile organizes these strategies into reusable components that can be retrieved, combined, and adapted to users' current tasks. Our evaluation shows that Promptile accurately recovers and reuses these strategies, better aligning model outputs with users' expectations while reducing repetitive prompting effort.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agent-OM: Leveraging LLM Agents for Ontology Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.00326v30)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ontology matching (OM) enables semantic interoperability between different ontologies and resolves their conceptual heterogeneity by aligning related entities. OM systems currently have two prevailing design paradigms: conventional knowledge-based expert systems and newer machine learning-based predictive systems. While large language models (LLMs) and LLM agents have revolutionised data engineering and have been applied creatively in many domains, their potential for OM remains underexplored. This study introduces a novel agent-powered LLM-based design paradigm for OM systems. With consideration of several specific challenges in leveraging LLM agents for OM, we propose a generic framework, namely Agent-OM (Agent for Ontology Matching), consisting of two Siamese agents for retrieval and matching, with a set of OM tools. Our framework is implemented in a proof-of-concept system. Evaluations of three Ontology Alignment Evaluation Initiative (OAEI) tracks over state-of-the-art OM systems show that our system can achieve results very close to the long-standing best performance on simple OM tasks and can significantly improve the performance on complex and few-shot OM tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>35 pa...</summary><p>35 pages - VLDB 2025 (Page 1-20), OM 2025 (Page 21-31), OM 2026 (Page 32-35)</p></details> |
| **[VerusEval: Evaluating LLM-Generated Verus Specifications Beyond Verifier Acceptance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26464v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate Verus contracts for Rust programs together with the proofs that establish them. Evaluating 13,716 artifacts from four workflows, five LLMs, and two prompting settings on 762 tasks, we find that acceptance overstates quality: only 42.3\% of the accepted artifacts complete all three semantic checks without a flag, and across workflows and models, higher acceptance does not consistently come with more correct contracts. Of the accepted artifacts flagged by contract comparison, 84.1\% have a rejected completeness obligation, and the direction of a failure prioritizes inspection of the generated contract or the reference; it does not by itself establish which artifact is defective. Accepted at NeurIPS 2026(spotlight)</p></details> |

Our results show that, on average, CSNB retains 99% of the fault detection capability of ABFT for the evaluated linear layers in dense LLMs, while reducing verification overhead by 87% and threshold determination overhead by 67%. For Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) router gate computations, however, CSNB retains only 55% of the fault detection capability of ABFT. These findings suggest that CSNB is a promising low-overhead alternative for protecting linear layers in dense LLMs, while checksum-based verification remains preferable for the evaluated MoE router gate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Productive State-Space Exploration for Multi-turn RL in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25957v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Large Language Model (LLM) agents in multi-turn, long-horizon environments remains challenging due to inefficient action selection and repetitive behavioral patterns. In addition, repetitive high-confidence actions lead to cyclical behaviors that provide little to no progress towards the goal. We argue that addressing these challenges requires more productive task-aligned exploration to improve the agent's understanding of environment dynamics. Motivated by intrinsic curiosity-driven exploration, we propose a conditional state-space exploration framework that balances task completion with intrinsic information-seeking behavior. Specifically, we introduce an intrinsic reward that penalizes actions whose observation duplicates prior state memory. This provides the benefit of reducing redundant behaviors and encouraging state-space coverage without relying on a general information-theoretic measure. Strong experimental results demonstrate that our approach substantially improves policy learning in multi-turn environments, mitigating invalid actions and repetitive loops.</p></details> |  |
| **[CXBench: A Benchmark for Process-Compliant LLM Agents in Regulated Customer-Experience Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25954v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using language agents increasingly perform regulated customer-service workflows, where a correct final state is insufficient: agents must also provide disclosures, obtain consent, and follow required procedures. Each task combines a customer request, a tool-based environment, and explicit process requirements. Rule cards link cited regulatory obligations to observable trace evidence. | **[AdaExploreBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Proactive Exploration and Test-time Adaptation in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25950v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. Under either reward, multinomial resampling can disrupt this balance by duplicating high-weight particles and discarding lineages that may still reach a correct solution. To retain such lineages, we introduce lineage-preserving SMC (LP-SMC), which combines Chopthin resampling with an input weight floor. Instead of equalizing weights, Chopthin bounds the ratio between the largest and smallest weights it returns and carries the unequal weights forward. The floor limits how far below the maximum a weight may fall before resampling so that lineages with low early weight keep a chance to recover. On the other hand, classical search algorithms have the potential to solve the planning problems perfectly, yet they require fully observable environments, known state transitions and goal state, which are often unattainable in general task domains. Our method leverages LLMs to (1) translate natural language instructions into formatted goal conditions, (2) facilitate efficient exploration in partially observable environments, (3) implement a rule-based world simulator, and (4) prune the space of candidate actions to improve search efficiency. | **[Budgeted Attack Scheduling for LLM Red-Teaming: The Roles of Static Priors, Semantic Guidance, and Online Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25897v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As jailbreak attacks against large language models (LLMs) become increasingly diverse, red-teaming has come to rely on large libraries of jailbreak prompts and attack strategies to evaluate the robustness of victim models and their defense settings. However, attack effectiveness is highly condition-dependent: an attack that succeeds under one victim-defense setting may fail under another, while exhaustively evaluating the full attack library for each setting incurs substantial token and monetary costs. This motivates studying how jailbreak attacks should be prioritized under a limited attempt budget for efficient robustness evaluation. In this work, we formalize this problem as budgeted attack scheduling over a fixed attack library and investigate three complementary signals under a fixed victim-defense condition: (1) static historical attack success rate (ASR) priors; (2) semantic guidance from LLMs; and (3) online feedback from earlier queries. Building on these signals, we design four scheduling strategies and evaluate them across three victim models and three defense settings on GPT-5.2. Extensive experiments clearly characterize the conditions under which each signal is most effective. Historical ASR provides a strong initialization, but its reliability degrades under defense-induced distribution shifts. Semantic guidance consistently improves or matches static prior-based scheduling in the cold-start regime, while online feedback becomes particularly valuable when transferred priors become stale. Specifically, a Role-conditioned Relational Attention module dynamically selects variable-cardinality interaction partners based on agent roles and evolving execution states, while a Topological Attention Mixer explicitly embeds the directed communication topology into team value decomposition for precise credit assignment. Comprehensive evaluations across 9 challenging benchmarks covering code generation, multi-step reasoning, general knowledge, and competitive mathematics demonstrate that ACTS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, boosting average accuracy by up to 6.19 percentage points while reducing token consumption by over 15%. Across all 20 models, unsafe-action rates are higher when no safe path for request fulfillment is available than when one is; for frontier models, the rates are 27-40% versus 5-7%. When a safe path exists, frontier models rarely refuse; when none exists, they most often propose an alternative. Open-weight models, by contrast, more often execute the request than propose alternatives and act unsafely on 58-83% of items. | **[When to Stop Asking: Factor Coverage Bias and LLM Determinability Judgments with Underspecified Information in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25819v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical decision-making often begins with underspecified information and requires not only gathering missing information but also knowing when to stop. Reliance on this amount as a cue for determinability, which we term \textit{factor coverage bias}, could undermine the trustworthiness of large language models (LLMs) through premature conclusions or excessive information gathering, such as unnecessary invasive tests. Yet whether LLMs exhibit this bias remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we construct a controlled pairwise benchmark in which LLMs identify the determinable case within each determinable--undeterminable pair. We add a decision-relevant factor to either side while keeping each case's determinability unchanged, and similarly add irrelevant text to distinguish this bias from context-length effects. Adding a relevant factor made LLMs more likely to select the augmented case as determinable even when it remained undeterminable, an effect that persisted with increased reasoning effort, while adding irrelevant text made them less likely to select the augmented case, an effect that reasoning substantially reduced. These findings identify factor coverage bias as a failure mode distinct from context-length effects and a potential risk to LLM trustworthiness. These neurons cluster in the query and value projections of later layers and exhibit highly asymmetric cross-category transferability. Furthermore, targeted fine-tuning of these neurons substantially improves fairness while preserving general capabilities. Importantly, models tuned with I-CAN-Debias generalize robustly across diverse evaluations beyond the training distribution, spanning open-ended fairness, jailbreak attacks, and implicit bias, without requiring test-time debiasing prompts. | **[Service-Induced Congestion in Memory-Constrained LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15555v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In large language model (LLM) serving, each request accumulates persistent graphics processing unit (GPU) memory during service as its key-value cache grows with every generated token. When memory capacity is exceeded, systems evict active requests, discarding cached state and restarting them later, which wastes computation and reduces throughput. In the saturated-input regime, the system admits both eviction-free fixed points and limit cycles with evictions. For homogeneous workloads, we show that the eviction-free equilibrium is unstable and that, except for a Lebesgue-measure-zero exact-capture set, the system converges to a unique worst-case limit cycle that is asymptotically stable outside this exceptional set, with throughput losses as large as 50%. For heterogeneous workloads, we prove a stability criterion in the two-class common-input setting and explain how the survival-polynomial mechanism generalizes to multiple classes and heterogeneous-input lengths. Under an input-dominated scaling regime, coprime decoding lengths stabilize the eviction-free equilibrium, while non-coprime lengths create synchronized modes that drive instability. These results characterize when workload heterogeneity desynchronizes completions and helps stabilize memory-constrained serving. More broadly, we identify service-induced congestion as a structural instability mechanism and derive scheduling design principles for sustaining high throughput.</p></details> | <details><summary>100 p...</summary><p>100 pages. v2: adds GPU experiments with benchmark workloads (Appendix D.4) and revises the figures</p></details> |
| **[How Much Does One Wrong Answer Cost? Error Propagation in Constraint Pruning with Noisy LLM Oracles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25739v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Constraint pruning saves queries to a large language model (LLM) or another noisy oracle by skipping each yes/no question implied by answers accepted earlier, for example through transitivity. Entity-resolution systems and LLM-based rankers use this idea. However, their analyses typically assume a correct oracle. We argue that pruning should be judged by its risk when the oracle errs, since an accepted wrong answer spreads to every question it implies. When each answer is wrong independently with small probability $\varepsilon$, the first-order risk is set by how many facts one wrong answer corrupts on average. We consider two kinds of constraints. For implication rules, as in taxonomies, pruning in a random order adds no first-order risk: a wrong answer that would change $k$ facts is accepted with probability $1/k$, so it costs one fact on average. Rules with $s$ premises break this balance: a wrong answer at a premise shared by $r$ rules can cost order $r^{1-1/s}$ facts. For entity resolution with $m$ records of one entity, the first-order risk equals $\varepsilon$ times the Wiener index (the total pairwise distance) of the spanning tree built by the query order. Pivot orders, which first compare one record with all others, amplify errors by less than 2 and are optimal; random orders by order $m^{1/3}$; the common most-similar-first order is the worst in one dimension and, for large $m$, worse than random in two. With several entities, the cost of wrongly merging two does not depend on how many others exist. Simulations agree with every predicted constant within sampling error.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, including appendices</p></details> |

A reservation scheduler commits each circuit while the prefill is still computing, so the circuit is ready when the KV cache is, and each GPU reaches the OCS through its own wavelength-multiplexed (WDM) optics on one fiber pair. In simulations of GPU clusters on real serving traces with prefill times measured on H100 GPUs, this optical KV cache network matches 400G packet fabrics with adaptive routing at 0.55 to 0.78 times the cost of a 2:1 fabric and tolerates the 50 ms reconfiguration delay of a 384-port mechanical OCS.</p></details> |  |
| **[Where and Why Search-Augmented LLMs Fail When Reasoning over Rapidly Evolving Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25323v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As the world changes, new information is published while older records remain online. To address this gap, we introduce TREK, a suite of three components: TREKTRACE is a plug-and-play framework that attributes failures to specific stages of retrieval and reasoning. We apply it to TREKBENCH-WILD, a meta-benchmark of real-world questions that require complex reasoning over rapidly evolving knowledge. Surprisingly, the main bottleneck is post-retrieval reasoning rather than retrieval: frontier models are often misled by conflicting evidence even after retrieving all the information needed to answer. To study this bottleneck, we build TREKBENCH-CTRL, a controlled benchmark with distractors spanning temporality, relevance, and trustworthiness, and show that benchmarks dominated by outdated distractors substantially underestimate task difficulty. Together, TREK provides a testbed for diagnosing and improving reasoning over rapidly evolving knowledge.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Double-Edged Sword of Open-Ended Interaction: How LLM-Driven NPCs Affect Players' Cognitive Load and Gaming Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10107v3)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study examines how large language model-driven non-player characters (LLM-NPCs) affect players' cognitive load and gaming experience, with a particular focus on the underlying psychological mechanisms, differences across task scenarios, and the role of individual traits. The outcomes vary with correct-answer availability. When a correct peer answer is available, correction exceeds harmful adoption after accounting for peer support in the primary setting. | **[Complementary Vulnerabilities: Auditing and Governing Clinician-LLM Error Overlap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25279v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Health systems are deploying large language models (LLMs) into clinical workflows, often on the assumption that a second automated check can only reduce error. We therefore propose a predeployment audit in which each site measures error overlap on its own cases. We also propose a governance model with which the site keeps those errors independent over time. Accepted at SMASH 2026, the Symposium on Model Accountability, Sustainability and Healthcare (Mila, Montreal, November 3-4, 2026), after peer review on OpenReview</p></details> |

| **[gKSM: GPU Memory Deduplication at Driver Layer for Serverless LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25219v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In serverless LLM inference, cloud providers commonly locate multiple instances on a single GPU to improve utilization. | **[Uber-Join: Unifying Diverse Table Joins using LLMs with Precision and Cost Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25178v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When two tables from different sources are joined, the values that should join often differ in spelling, format, or meaning, and fuzzy-, transformation-, and semantic-joins each handle one type of these differences. Our key idea is to use LLMs selectively to create join programs and verify their outputs through sampling, while adaptively deciding which joins to pursue under budget and precision constraints. This combines the scalability of program-based joins with selective LLM verification, while maintaining a statistical precision guarantee on the accumulated result. We develop Uber-Join, which treats creating a join and checking the output of a created join as two levels of arms in a budgeted multi-armed bandit. It commits an output only if the precision of the whole result, estimated from an LLM's judgments of sampled pairs, stays at or above the target with high confidence. Under the same budget, Uber-Join achieves the highest recall at the precision target in nearly all settings on three join benchmarks and Join-Hard, a new benchmark of large tables.</p></details> |  |

| **[MemBubble: Benchmarking How Agent Memory Shapes LLM Response Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25127v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory serves as the core component of LLM-based agents, enabling personalized responses via extracting and using user preferences from past interactions. | **[Empowering Edge Agents: A Systematic Analysis of Edge-Cloud Collaboration in LLM Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17816v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Edge-cloud collaboration combines the lower inference overhead and greater data locality of edge-side execution with the stronger capabilities of cloud-hosted models. However, as LLM agents increasingly operate in long-horizon scenarios, cloud participation becomes interleaved throughout execution rather than a one-shot decision for each query. Such interleaved participation introduces trajectory-level effects that remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we introduce EC2-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution. It provides executable digital-workspace agent tasks and a unified evaluation framework covering task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure. Using this benchmark, we empirically study diverse forms of edge-cloud collaboration. Our study reveals three findings. First, collaboration achieves a favorable balance among task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure compared with edge-only and cloud-only execution. Second, as edge models become stronger, collaborative execution approaches cloud-only performance, and performance differences across strategies shrink, while privacy exposure remains strongly strategy-dependent. Third, stronger edge capability shifts preferred collaboration from externally controlled cloud participation and direct cloud execution toward more edge-controlled collaboration. Further analyses identify cloud-invocation decision-making and instruction following as important edge-side abilities for effective collaboration. These findings provide a systematic understanding of edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution and insights for designing future edge agents that effectively collaborate with cloud models. | **[ClinSDT: LLM-Encoded Clinical Semantic Guidance for Sepsis Treatment via Decision Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25121v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline sepsis treatment aims to learn treatment policies from retrospective ICU trajectories. | **[Memory as Belief: Bayesian Evolution of Memory for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25119v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to accumulate past experiences for continual adaptation. Inspired by the Bayesian brain hypothesis, we propose BEAM (Bayesian-style Evolutionary Agentic Memory), a framework that reformulates memory value evaluation as a parameterized cognitive inference process, where memory value is represented as a context-conditioned dynamic belief rather than a passive historical statistic. Specifically, BEAM infers memory value beliefs from current task contexts and continuously calibrates them using execution feedback as empirical evidence, transforming memory from passive storage into an adaptive cognitive mechanism for continual self-evolution. Extensive experiments across reasoning and code generation benchmarks demonstrate that BEAM consistently improves memory utilization and enables continual self-evolution of LLM agents.</p></details> |  |

## graph neural network
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Controllable GNN Explanations via Multi-Metric Preference Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32436v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mechanisms for generating GNN explanations are crucial for building trust and mitigating biases in Graph Neural Networks (GNNs), especially in high-stakes scenarios. Most current methods optimize only for fidelity under the sparsity constraint. However, this discounts the need for interpretable explanations (those that consist of familiar motif patterns) and stable explanations (those that remain unchanged under structural perturbations). We propose a novel approach that optimizes GNN explanations across these metrics, exposing their relative weighing as a control. Experiments on various real-world datasets, including MUTAG, BA-2Motif, BAMultiShapes, and PROTEINS, suggest that our method produces higher-fidelity explanations than a state-of-the-art baseline on MUTAG and PROTEINS across all evaluated budgets, and on BA-2Motif at larger budgets, while being faster in the regime of small explanation budgets. They also indicate that our method produces more stable explanations for the MUTAG and BA-2Motif datasets. We also examine the relationship among different metrics via their induced trade-off surface and explore its dependence on the nature of the motif library.</p></details> |  |
| **[PhyPro-FM: A Physics and Prompt-Guided Graph Foundation Model for Contingency Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26351v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The continuous evolution of next-generation power systems, characterized by the high penetration of renewable energy resources and dynamic topology variations, poses formidable challenges to traditional N-1/N-2 contingency analysis in terms of computational efficiency and scalability. While deep learning-based methods offer accelerated inference, existing studies predominantly treat contingency analysis as a pure regression problem, often overlooking the physical essence of grid operation and engineering requirements of real-world scenarios. To bridge this gap, this paper proposes a physics and prompt-guided graph foundation model (PhyPro-FM) for generalizable and engineering-viable contingency analysis. The proposed architecture integrates graph neural networks with a learnable global query mechanism and, notably, introduces a prompt-guided learning paradigm. By leveraging historical snapshots as few-shot prompts, the model dynamically captures correlations between target and historical operating conditions, significantly enhancing generalization capabilities under unseen topologies. Furthermore, we design a physics-informed training mechanism that prioritizes branch power flow errors over nodal voltage errors, aligning the optimization objective with critical grid security constraints. To ensure engineering applicability, a physics-informed inference mechanism is also developed, incorporating a residual-based correction module and a specialized post-processing strategy for singular branches to circumvent numerical instability. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that PhyPro-FM outperforms state-of-the-art methods in accuracy and generalization, validating its superior physical consistency and robustness for practical engineering applications.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning SOCP Solver Dynamics with Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26285v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Second-order cone programs (SOCPs) are a fundamental class of convex optimization problems widely applied across engineering domains. Classical interior-point methods are robust and accurate, but their iterative nature can be costly in real-time or repeated-solve settings. This has motivated learning-based solvers that approximate optimizer solutions with neural networks. Recent work has increasingly studied graph neural networks (GNNs) for linear, quadratic, and second-order cone optimization, leveraging their ability to model sparse relational structure among variables, constraints, and objective functions. Most existing GNN-based solvers compress the optimization process into a single forward pass, predicting the optimizer directly without using the intermediate trajectory produced by classical solvers. We introduce SOCPNet, a recursive GNN framework that uses solver trajectories to learn an iterative SOCP solution procedure. A shared GNN model is applied repeatedly, refining the current iterate at each step and enabling inference for a variable number of iterations. Rather than predicting the solution in a single pass, SOCPNet learns from intermediate iterates to progressively refine its estimates using a trajectory-regularized training procedure. We evaluate SOCPNet on multiple datasets spanning standard SOCP problem classes and show that it consistently reduces the final solution error relative to a SOCP-GNN baseline. Finally, we demonstrate how trained SOCPNet models can be used in a hybrid optimization pipeline by warm-starting a classical interior-point solver, reducing solver iterations without loss of accuracy.</p></details> | <details><summary>Learn...</summary><p>Learning on Graphs (LoG) Conference, 2026</p></details> |
| **[SIGMA: A Versatile Streaming Graph Partitioner for Vertex- and Edge-Balanced Distributed GNN Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03519v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distributed Graph Neural Network (GNN) training depends critically on how the underlying graph is partitioned across compute resources. Existing graph partitioners focus either on vertex partitioning or edge partitioning and typically optimize only a single communication objective (edge cut vertex cut) under a single balance constraint (vertex balance edge balance). We present SIGMA (Streaming Integrated Graph Partitioning with Multi-objective Awareness), a versatile streaming graph partitioner that supports both vertex and edge partitioning within a unified multi-objective, multi-constraint framework. Depending on the target distributed GNN system, SIGMA can be configured for edge-cut-oriented vertex partitioning or vertex-cut-oriented edge partitioning while simultaneously accounting for vertex and edge balancing. A clustering-based preprocessing stage incorporates global graph structure to improve partition quality while preserving the efficiency and scalability advantages of streaming partitioning. We evaluate SIGMA on six benchmark graphs spanning diverse domains and scales using two distributed GNN training systems: DistGNN (edge-partitioned) and DistDGL (vertex-partitioned). Across both settings, SIGMA consistently achieves strong performance, showing its ability to navigate complex trade-offs between partition quality, training efficiency, and memory consumption, frequently outperforming established streaming approaches and delivering competitive results against high-quality in-memory partitioners, with particularly strong results in edge-partitioned GNN training. These results demonstrate that a unified streaming partitioner can effectively address the communication, compute, and memory challenges of distributed GNN training across fundamentally different system architectures.</p></details> |  |
| **[On Counting Graphs for Modal Logic with Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26187v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we study the theoretical computational complexity of counting trees and graphs that are classified positively by a graph neural networks, or said equivalently that satisfy a given formula of modal logic with counting. We also study convergence.</p></details> |  |
| **[Heterogeneous Sheaf Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.08036v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Heterogeneous graphs, whose nodes and edges can belong to different types and feature spaces, arise in many real-world domains, including biology, recommendation, social networks, and computer systems. To formally analyze the expressive power of TGNNs, we lack a generalization of graph isomorphism to temporal graphs that fully captures their causal topology. Addressing this gap, we introduce the notion of consistent event graph isomorphism, which utilizes a time-unfolded representation of time-respecting paths in temporal graphs. We compare this definition with existing notions of temporal graph isomorphisms. We illustrate and highlight the advantages of our approach and develop a temporal generalization of the Weisfeiler-Leman algorithm to heuristically distinguish non-isomorphic temporal graphs. Building on this theoretical foundation, we derive a novel message passing scheme for temporal graph neural networks that operates on the event graph representation of temporal graphs. An experimental evaluation shows that our approach performs well in a temporal graph classification experiment.</p></details> |  |

We are able to retrieve local stress field distributions, providing average stress values produced by a mean field reduced order model (ROM) or Finite Element (FE) simulation at the macro-scale. Our model incorporates physical constraints during training to constraint local stress field equilibrium state and employs a periodic graph representation to enforce periodic boundary conditions. The benefits of the proposed physics-informed GNN are evaluated considering linear and non linear hyperelastic responses applied to varying geometries. In the non-linear hyperelastic case, the proposed method achieves significant computational speed-ups compared to FE simulation, making it particularly attractive for large-scale applications.</p></details> |  |

This resolves the scale mismatch that prevents fixed-weight formulations from converging in the elasto-plastic regime. Towards explaining this phenomenon, we derive a bound on epistemic variance in terms of the graph topology and Dirichlet energy budget, linking epistemic collapse to the smoothing bias of message passing. We formalize this complementary notion as graph-structured mask dependence, where the joint distribution of missingness indicators is shaped by the graph, and introduce a controlled mechanism that instantiates this setting. We study propagation-based methods theoretically and empirically in this setting, showing that their effectiveness changes substantially compared with the uniformly random regime. Motivated by this limitation, we propose PEMix, designed to remain effective under challenging graph-structured missingness. Rather than propagating observed values, PEMix uses global structural representations to condition a probabilistic model of incomplete node features before message passing. | **[Beyond Parents? We find small overall prediction gaps because parental socioeconomic background captures most predictable variation; even GNNs add little once parents are known. Prediction gaps are largest among children without a registered father, especially girls and those with less-educated mothers. Methodologically, we argue that prediction gaps can support sociological theory-building: small gaps show where current theory-based models already explain what can be measured; large gaps identify where targeted mechanism-focused research is warranted.</p></details> |  |
| **[Chasing Label Shifters: A Change-Aware Framework for Dynamic Graph Node Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17582v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Entities in real-world systems often evolve over time: users shift between information consumption patterns, researchers migrate between fields, and firms transition between financial risk states. Our structural analysis traces this failure to embedding inertia: neighborhood aggregation keeps shifter representations anchored to the old-class centroid, and this effect is strongest precisely where it is hardest to correct, namely for shifters whose neighborhoods remain aligned with the old class. | **[Colored Graph Signal Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17282v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Standard diffusion processes with isotropic noise corruption are not spectrally neutral. They inherit a coarse-to-fine generation hierarchy from the nonuniform data spectrum, because each frequency band loses signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) at its own rate. For signals on a graph, the relevant spectrum is that of the graph shift operator (GSO). A natural construction, therefore, builds both the diffusion dynamics and the denoiser from graph filters of the GSO, and any such design also takes a position on this hierarchy. We expose this hierarchy as a tunable axis through a family of colored score-based diffusions. Fixing the terminal prior to a Gaussian colored by the GSO couples the drift and diffusion through a positive spectral rate, which acts as a clock for each graph-frequency band. The clock sets when a band loses and recovers information, and the forward processes of standard and certain graph-aware diffusions appear as members of this family. A GSO-valued EDM preconditioning splits the denoiser into an analytic Wiener head that carries the Gaussian second-order structure and a graph neural network (GNN) that learns the remaining non-Gaussian dependencies. Numerical experiments show that colored diffusions reach a given generation quality in far fewer sampling steps than standard diffusion.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submission under review for ICASSP 2027</p></details> |

| **[Imitation Learning of Avoidance Modes for Efficient Multimodal Model Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16326v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal model predictive control (MM-MPC) avoids local minima among moving obstacles by solving one optimal control problem (OCP) per combination of avoidance sides, so its computational cost grows exponentially with the number of decision-critical obstacles. A graph neural network is trained to predict the expert's minimum-cost mode, one avoidance side per obstacle. Its edges encode, for each obstacle pair, the gap predicted for the moment the vehicle first reaches either obstacle, so a neighbor that blocks a preferred passage can reverse the proposed side. The expert's selection rule compares its minimum-cost mode with the previously executed mode; SideNet-MPC keeps this rule and substitutes the network proposal for the minimum-cost mode. Hence at most two OCPs are solved per control period, and every applied input still comes from an OCP with control barrier function constraints rather than from the network. In $100$ seed-matched closed-loop Monte Carlo runs of mixed traffic, SideNet-MPC reaches $93\%$ of the expert's success rate and needs \SI{18.6}{ms} per control period on a single thread, 48\% less than the expert's \SI{35.5}{ms} on sixteen parallel threads. | **[A Dependency-Aware Multi-GPU Training Framework for Temporal Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15730v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Temporal graph neural networks (TGNNs) achieve superior performance in many dynamic graph learning tasks. These models use node state vectors (i.e., memories) to capture and record graph changes (i.e., events), and then exploit these memories to assist prediction. However, this memory-centric design makes multi-GPU training challenging because predictions for later events often depend on memory updates from earlier events. To maintain model quality, existing multi-GPU TGNN training schemes strictly preserve all event dependencies, which limits parallelism and causes expensive cross-GPU memory synchronization. In this work, we observe that many of these dependencies can be handled more flexibly without harming training quality. Specifically, (a) dependencies in TGNN training have high locality in both time and graph topology, (b) the short-range dependencies missed within a large batch can often be recovered cheaply, and (c) many memory updates are not immediately needed. Based on these observations, we propose DepBloom to accelerate multi-GPU TGNN training with three techniques: (1) temporally bounded spatial partitioning to reduce unnecessary cross-GPU synchronization and improve parallelism; (2) intra-batch memory refinement to recover missing short-range temporal dependencies inside enlarged batches; and (3) critical-path optimization to prioritize urgent memory updates while deferring non-critical work during multi-GPU pipeline execution. Experimental results across representative models and diverse datasets show that DepBloom achieves an average speedup of 2.84$\times$ and up to 5.16$\times$ over state-of-the-art methods, while maintaining comparable or better model accuracy.</p></details> |  |
| **[Teaching MLP More Graph Information: A Three-stage Multitask Knowledge Distillation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.01079v2)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study the challenging problem for inference tasks on large-scale graph datasets of Graph Neural Networks: huge time and memory consumption, and try to overcome it by reducing reliance on graph structure. Even though distilling graph knowledge to student MLP is an excellent idea, it faces two major problems of positional information loss and low generalization. To solve the problems, we propose a new three-stage multitask distillation framework. In detail, we use Positional Encoding to capture positional information. Also, we introduce Neural Heat Kernels responsible for graph data processing in GNN and utilize hidden layer outputs matching for better performance of student MLP's hidden layers. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first work to include hidden layer distillation for student MLP on graphs and to combine graph Positional Encoding with MLP. We test its performance and robustness with several settings and draw the conclusion that our work can outperform well with good stability. Code implementation is in https://github.com/lijunxian111/KMP.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, with Appendix</p></details> |
| **[An Attention-Guided Graph Neural Network Surrogate for Boundary Integral Computation in Physical Field Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15590v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In practical engineering and scientific computing, a real physical system can be reduced to a boundary value problem, and the adequate boundary information to determine the interior spatial distribution and evolutionary state of the physical field can be solved with linear equations, alternatively, the the real physical problem can be treated with boundary problems with in adequate information problems in field of machine learning. This paper proposes a deep learning framework for the recovery of missing boundary information using an attention-guided graph neural network (GNN) surrogate. The framework extracts features from the physical information on the discretized boundary into a latent space, and the missing boundary quantities are then predicted by the network. Thus, the nonlinear mapping from known boundary quantities to missing boundary quantities is learned using a variational autoencoder (VAE) architecture, while an attention mechanism is incorporated to better model the correlations among different types of boundary information. Furthermore, the interior physical field can be reconstructed using the conventional boundary element method (BEM) integral formulation. The effectiveness of the GNN-BEM framework is evaluated using numerical examples of the two-dimensional Laplace and Poisson equations, which demonstrate good performance in reconstruction accuracy, local detail preservation, and model stability. The results show that GNN-BEM provides a feasible computational framework for boundary information recovery and fast interior physical field reconstruction for partial differential equations under complex boundary conditions.</p></details> |  |
| **[ATLAS: A Foundation Neural Sampler for Amorphous Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19198v2)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Amorphous materials exhibit exceptional mechanical and functional properties, yet their rugged energy landscapes are notoriously difficult to sample. Below the glass-transition temperature, conventional molecular dynamics and Monte Carlo become inefficient because equilibration relies on rare barrier-crossing events, while data-driven generative models are constrained by scarce and biased reference ensembles. Here, we introduce ATLAS, an efficient sampler that learns a diffusion process to generate Boltzmann-distributed amorphous structures directly from a target energy function. Parameterized by an equivariant graph neural network, ATLAS generalizes across system size, temperature, and composition. By exploiting the time reversal of the diffusion process, it enables efficient estimation of thermodynamic quantities and steering toward target observables. In two-dimensional Kob-Andersen systems, ATLAS reproduces parallel tempering Markov chain Monte Carlo structural distributions, free energies and entropies, achieving below 0.2% free energy error in the low-temperature glass regime with over 500-fold fewer energy evaluations. In Cu-Zr and Cr-Co-Ni metallic glasses, ATLAS recovers experimentally observed short-range-order trends and steers structures toward prescribed order parameters and optimized bulk moduli. Moreover, composition-amortized pretraining outperforms composition-specific training from scratch, reduces inverse-design costs by several hundred-fold, and enables sampling with expensive universal machine learning interatomic potentials. Coupled to a large language model agent, ATLAS searches an eight-element space for high-entropy metallic glasses balancing stiffness and ductility, identifying a converged Pareto frontier within 480 oracle evaluations. Together, these results establish ATLAS as a foundation model for sampling, steering and designing amorphous materials.</p></details> |  |
| **[Sequence-Structured Decision Learning for Branch-and-Bound in Mixed-Integer Linear Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14835v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Branch-and-bound (BnB) solves mixed-integer linear programs by constructing and exploring a search tree. Node selection and branching are two recurrent decisions that directly determine the tree. Learning-based approaches typically optimize either decision in isolation and condition on a mostly local view, leaving the coupled evolution of the search tree insufficiently modeled. This paper proposes Seq-BnB, a sequence-structured framework that learns branching and node selection from a shared global representation of the evolving BnB process. Seq-BnB serializes node creations, node updates, node-selection events, and branching events into typed tokens, producing a causal event sequence of the search trajectory. A self-attention encoder summarizes this global search history, a bipartite graph neural network encodes the current LP relaxation, and context-conditioned decision heads score branching variables and open nodes. To reduce distribution shift between expert demonstrations and learned policies, Seq-BnB is trained with DAgger-style data aggregation. On four MILP benchmark families, Seq-BnB consistently processes fewer BnB nodes than heuristic and learning-based baselines, with strong solution times on both standard and larger transfer instances. While this paper evaluates Seq-BnB instantiation for branching and node selection, it accommodates additional solver decisions, such as cut selection, by introducing new action tokens and decision heads.</p></details> |  |
| **[GREAT: A Graph Edge Attention Network for the Electric Vehicle Routing Problem with Asymmetric Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.16717v4)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Due to the ongoing electrification of transportation systems, energy-based routing problems such as the Electric Vehicle Routing Problem (EVRP) are becoming increasingly important. Unlike Euclidean routing problems, the EVRP involves asymmetric energy costs caused by road topology. Existing learning-based routing approaches are based on graph neural networks (GNNs) or transformer architectures operating on Euclidean node coordinates, making them poorly suited for asymmetric, non-Euclidean edge-based metrics such as energy consumption. To address this, we propose the Graph Edge Attention Network (GREAT), a novel GNN-related architecture that operates purely in edge space rather than on node coordinates, making it naturally compatible with asymmetric routing costs. Integrated into an end-to-end reinforcement learning framework, GREAT jointly optimizes routing and charging decisions for the EVRP. We train the model using real-world energy consumption data from San Francisco, derived from physical road networks enriched with topological features, and evaluate generalization on datasets from San Francisco, Vienna, and Gothenburg. Across diverse battery sizes (15-40 kWh) and customer demand distributions, GREAT outperforms a strong HGS-based EVRP heuristic in most tested settings, achieving energy reductions of up to 8.78% while reducing runtime by a factor of 60. Moreover, we benchmark against a custom adaptive large neighborhood search (ALNS) that jointly optimizes routing and charging. GREAT achieves competitive performance at a faster runtime, and we show that the ALNS can profit from a warm start with the GREAT solution. We further evaluate GREAT on standard (asymmetric) routing problems, including Traveling Salesman Problem, Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem, and Orienteering Problem, where it achieves state-of-the-art performance among neural methods for non-Euclidean settings.</p></details> | 40 pages, 10 figures |
| **[DeKoR: Decomposed Knowledge Routing for Efficient GNN--LLM Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14710v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong potential for text-attributed graph learning by leveraging rich pre-trained knowledge and language understanding capabilities. Combining LLMs with Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) offers a promising paradigm for expanding the modeling capabilities of graph learning. However, LLMs and GNNs follow distinct learning paradigms, leading to a pronounced mismatch between their knowledge spaces. Such discrepancies make effective knowledge fusion nontrivial. Moreover, existing methods often incur substantial additional computation for only limited performance gains. To address these challenges, we propose DeKoR, an efficient framework for GNN and LLM knowledge fusion. To enable efficient knowledge transfer, DeKoR decomposes LLM knowledge into complementary local and global views and uses the LLM as an offline teacher to distill them into lightweight experts. To enable selective knowledge fusion, an adaptive router learns how much each node should rely on each expert. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that DeKoR outperforms existing baselines on both node and subgraph classification, achieving superior effectiveness and efficiency.</p></details> | 19 pages |
| **[Amortized Counterfactual Prediction with Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13724v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Counterfactual inference asks how an observed outcome might have differed under different circumstances, such as whether a patient would have recovered had they received a different treatment. Such questions arise whenever decisions depend on the outcomes of alternatives that were not observed. Answering counterfactual queries generally requires knowledge of the underlying causal structure, which is often unavailable in practice. A common approach is therefore to first estimate the causal graph from observational data and then perform counterfactual inference. However, errors in the estimated causal graph can propagate to downstream counterfactual predictions. To address this challenge, we introduce the Counterfactual Graph Neural Process (CFGNP), an end-to-end approach that answers counterfactual queries directly from observational data without first estimating a causal graph. CFGNP uses Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to exploit the dual-graph structure by restricting cross-world message passing to corresponding factual--counterfactual variables. Across synthetic and image-based benchmarks, CFGNP achieves stronger predictive performance than existing approaches, including on the German Loan benchmark, where it reduces MSE from $4.51 \times 10^{-2}$ to $0.19 \times 10^{-2}$. Furthermore, on synthetic causal graphs, a model trained only on 10-node graphs maintains strong predictive performance on graphs with up to 30 nodes, without retraining.</p></details> |  |
| **[Hierarchical Graph Neural Network for Probabilistic Power Flow with Grid Topology Contingencies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13495v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Data-driven surrogates reduce the nonlinear solves required for probabilistic power flow (PPF), but models tied to a fixed bus--branch graph often generalize poorly when outages change electrical coupling. This paper proposes a topology-decomposition-driven hierarchical bidirectional graph neural network (TDD--HBGNN) for PPF under unseen topologies. For each realized topology, the network is decomposed into fundamental topological units (FTUs), dynamically homogeneous voltage clusters (DHVCs), and a core backbone sub-grid (CBSG), with equivalent-admittance representations preserving electrical ports between units. A sensitivity-based linear prior captures the first-order voltage-magnitude and phase-angle response, while the HBGNN learns topology-dependent nonlinear residuals. Impedance- and uncertainty-aware bottom-up aggregation condenses local perturbations, while mode-modulated top-down broadcasting restores system context at buses. We evaluate our approach on the IEEE 118-bus and Polish 2746-bus systems under unseen N--1 and N--2 contingencies against six baseline methods. Compared with these methods, TDD--HBGNN achieves 2.47--4.20-fold lower all-bus phase-angle errors and lower phase-angle errors in every held-out event. Its voltage-magnitude advantage is concentrated near the outaged branches, where paired error ratios reach 1.75--2.58 on the IEEE system and 1.87--1.97 in Polish N--2 events. All system--contingency--baseline comparisons favor TDD--HBGNN in joint voltage--angle error. Moreover, shared hierarchical operators keep the model size at 0.658 million parameters across both grids and yield 0.161--1.793~ms forward latency per probabilistic case. These results demonstrate system-wide angular accuracy, outage-local voltage accuracy, and parameter-efficient scaling across unseen topologies.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multi-Modal Graph Neural Network-Inspired Vessel Identification and Tracking for UAV-Based Maritime Surveillance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13220v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV)-based maritime surveillance is becoming an important part of maritime Internet of Things (IoT) and intelligent transportation systems. It provides a flexible and cost-effective way to large-scale maritime traffic monitoring. However, achieving robust situational awareness remains challenging in practical maritime environments. The main challenges arise from heterogeneous sensing modalities, adverse imaging conditions, and spatial misalignment caused by UAV motion. To address these problems, this paper proposes an airborne multi-modal graph learning-driven framework, termed AMGT, for vessel identification and tracking in UAV-assisted maritime IoT. Specifically, visible and infrared images are first aligned and fused through a multi-parameter perspective transformation model. This improves visual perception under low visibility and complex illumination conditions. Automatic identification system (AIS) messages are then temporally synchronized using interpolation and linear prediction. To detect small and oriented vessels, we develop a mixed-modality MAir-YOLOv8-OBB detector, followed by the MAir-SORT algorithm for stable vessel tracking. We further design a graph learning-driven association network to model temporal dynamics and spatial dependencies among vessels and between vessels and UAVs. A motion-aware coordinate unification (MCU) strategy is also introduced to reduce nonlinear misalignment caused by UAV motion. Experiments on the MAP-M2UAV50 dataset show that AMGT achieves high accuracy and efficiency. The results demonstrate its potential for UAV-assisted maritime IoT applications, including vessel monitoring, traffic situational awareness, and intelligent maritime transportation services. The dataset and code will be available at https://github.com/MAPGroup/AMGT.</p></details> |  |
| **[Mesh-Native Tokenisation: Retrofitting Foundation Models to Unstructured Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12618v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Physics foundation models are an increasingly popular approach for building versatile emulators for spatiotemporal systems. They require tokenisation layers that lift physical field data into a latent representation suitable for dynamic processing. A typical choice for this layer is a convolutional encoder, which lifts field information in a locality-aware way and can be trained very efficiently. However, this choice cannot be applied to a large class of scientific computing problems where data are represented on unstructured meshes, for example simulations on domains with curved boundaries. Such data typically require graph neural network architectures, which are more expensive to train and, in the form currently used, encode information differently from their CNN counterparts, thus limiting the portability of physics emulators between these data regimes. In this work we introduce Mesh-Native Convolution (MNC), a locality-aware tokenisation layer that encodes unstructured mesh-based data in a way that is analogous to CNNs. This approach leads to models that can be trained once on mesh data and then deployed to arbitrary unstructured meshes, even if these are irregular, with minimal retraining. We show that this tokenisation approach can be used to retrofit grid-pretrained foundation models efficiently on unstructured meshes and provide theoretical results to confirm the consistency of this approach with classical CNN encoders. We demonstrate the favourable performance of this retrofitting approach, which in many cases reaches acceptable accuracy even in zero-shot deployment, and outperforms existing baselines after only a few epochs of fine-tuning, thus providing an efficient bridge between the two data regimes.</p></details> |  |
